Ready when you are to discuss the Membrane Map implementation plan proposed at the end of yesterday's chat. The plan as proposed consists of:

- **10-rung zipper** (pure math → DOM → integration → E2E), organized by confidence level
- **5 dev-day sequence** (A: layout + hierarchy, B: detail levels + edge bundling, C: Browse + Explore rendering, D: Compare + Path + integration, E: phase-out + docs)
- **6 module promotions** as a preparatory refactor (squarify, connection-geometry, pan-zoom, card-factory, symbol-highlight, runtime)
- **Target directory**: `membraneView/` with ~20 files replacing `circuitView/` (9) + `localView/` (24), net −11 view files
- **Open question**: Browse/Explore mode naming still unsettled

What would you like to discuss, refine, or challenge?

Made changes.

User: Thank you for a thorough initial response! The only thing I would challenge is the "5 day" portion of the "5 day sequence". I think we should perhaps frame it as a "5 major step sequence" or something closer to that. The only reason being that temporality is very very very warped for LLMs, and time estimates from LLMs are nearly guaranteed to be incorrect due to the very nature of response generation via LLMs.

What I'd ask is that you encode this plan into a markdown doc in your temp directory (please feel free to do some cleaning in there; it's getting to be a mess) that we can then execute off of, allowing the development to be initialized and proceed over quite a few context windows without issue. That markdown doc for the plan will feature stateful checklist-like items to ensure that it's easy to pick up where you left off if autosummarizations trim some context you needed. It is expected that implementation will show us new roadblocks and fork-in-the-road decisions. If those issues arise, you are expected to pause and discuss so that we can sharpen the design further. We should expect this to happen at least once during the execution of development, as no plan is ever perfect. As such, we should expect the plan file itself to change at least once during execution to accommodate updated decisions/understandings/realities.

Therefore, the point at which I'd like you to end your next response is when you encounter the first roadblock or fork-in-the-road decision. We are in agreement with how to proceed, so you can begin with high autonomy.

User: There is substantially more rigmarole in a commit than you likely think. You would need to generate the new Live Documentation files for the new implementation files, delete all the Live Documentation files that correspond to files we deleted (the generation step will refuse to delete any live documentation file which contains authored content). Once the new live documentation files are in place, we have to go through the process of populating their authored content sections, and the process is much more context-intensive than you typically predict (I really should write a reusable prompt for that part).

And that's just the live documentation file generation part. Then we have to pass the entirety of an `npm run safe:commit` check, which often finds broken markdown links which signal to us that we are not clear to commit according to our own standards.

All of that is to say: Get the implementation where you want it, prove that it works, and _then_ do the remaining commit prep work. If the implementation is expected to continuosly deviate from commit to commit, then we will cause a lot of unnecessary churn in the new Live Documentation files and a lot of unnecessary additional updating work on those documlentation files, which could be populated well at the end of the process once we know the code is where we want it to be.

---

**Fork-in-the-road: What should Step 2 actually contain?**

The plan says Step 2 is "Hierarchy Builders" with three functions: `pathToHierarchy`, `namespaceToHierarchy`, and `isBarrelFile`. But looking at the actual codebase:

1. **`pathToHierarchy`** — This is `buildHierarchy()` in layoutUtils.ts. It already works, is already tested by the Circuit Board, and is already consumed by `computeMembraneLayout()` indirectly (the caller passes a `DirectoryNode` tree). There's nothing to build here — the Membrane Map layout engine receives a pre-built `DirectoryNode` tree and doesn't care how it was constructed.

2. **`namespaceToHierarchy`** — This requires namespace data. The C# adapter (`extractCSharpNamespace()` in csharp.ts) extracts namespace strings server-side, but that data never flows into `ExplorerNodePayload`. Implementing namespace mode requires:
   - Adding a `namespace?: string` field to `ExplorerNodePayload`
   - Plumbing it from the server through the explorer graph builder
   - Then writing the hierarchy function on the client

   This is LD-1207 in the backlog — a real feature, not a layout concern.

3. **`isBarrelFile` + `applyBarrelSemantics`** — This is genuinely needed and self-contained. Pure filename-based detection, no new data pipeline needed.

**The question for you**: Should Step 2 be scoped down to just barrel detection (`isBarrelFile` + `applyBarrelSemantics`), deferring namespace mode entirely to LD-1207? Or do you want to tackle the payload extension now as part of the Membrane Map foundation?

My recommendation is **scope down** — barrel detection is the concrete value here, and namespace mode is a separate data plumbing concern that can be wired in after the Membrane Map visualization is working end-to-end. The hierarchy function abstraction (path-based vs namespace-based) is already implicit: whoever calls `computeMembraneLayout()` can pass either a path-based or namespace-based `DirectoryNode` tree. The layout engine doesn't care.

I've already written the test file with a `buildNamespaceHierarchy` describe block — if we scope down, I'll remove that block and keep just `isBarrelFile` + `applyBarrelSemantics`.

**Fork-in-the-road: DOM testing infrastructure for Step 5+.**

The Vitest environment is configured as `"node"` — no DOM. The existing codebase has zero DOM-level tests; all view tests (like state.test.ts) test pure state logic, and all renderers use raw `document.createElement()` without a framework.

Step 5 (Browse Mode Rendering) is the first DOM rung. Three options:

1. **Install `jsdom`** (or `happy-dom`) as a devDependency and configure Vitest with a per-file `@vitest-environment jsdom` pragma. This lets DOM-rendering tests call `document.createElement()`, assert on `.className`, `.style`, `.children`, etc. Adds ~1 dependency, ~2 lines of config.

2. **Write renderers as pure data → element-descriptor mappings** (a lightweight "virtual DOM" layer), then test the descriptors without needing a real DOM. The actual DOM creation becomes a thin untested bridge. More architectural overhead, but zero new dependencies.

3. **Skip DOM unit tests entirely** — write renderers directly, rely on Playwright E2E in Step 10 for visual regression. Matches the existing codebase pattern (no DOM tests exist today). Faster to implement, less safety net during development.

My recommendation: **Option 1 (jsdom)**. It's the smallest change that gives us the most testing leverage. The existing pure-state tests keep running in Node, and renderer tests get a DOM. One `@vitest-environment jsdom` comment at the top of each DOM test file does the job without touching the global config.

Which direction should we take?

GitHub Copilot:

Good question. Let me be precise about what jsdom actually buys us here.

**Code duplication: effectively zero.** Tests call the same production renderer functions and inspect the output DOM. There's no parallel DOM-building code. So "duplication" isn't the risk.

**The real risk is _uselessness_.** Here's why:

The renderers will do something like:

```ts
const el = document.createElement("div");
el.className = "membrane";
el.style.left = `${node.rect.x}px`;
el.style.width = `${node.rect.width}px`;
el.dataset.id = node.id;
```

A jsdom test would assert:

```ts
expect(el.style.left).toBe("100px");
expect(el.className).toBe("membrane");
```

That's testing a trivial property-assignment bridge. Meanwhile:

- **The math that computes those coordinates is already tested** (Steps 1-4, 34 tests).
- **The things that actually break** — CSS cascade interactions, `getBoundingClientRect()` returning real measurements, pan/zoom reading viewport sizes, SVG paths depending on measured anchor positions — **jsdom can't test any of them** (it returns `{x:0, y:0, width:0, height:0}` for all layout queries).
- **The existing codebase confirms this**: Circuit Board and Local Map have **zero** DOM-level tests. All their tests are pure-state/pure-logic (like state.test.ts). Visual correctness is verified via Playwright E2E or manual inspection.

So jsdom tests would verify the one layer least likely to break (property assignment) while being structurally unable to verify the layers most likely to break (visual layout, CSS, measurements). They'd pass even when the UI is completely wrong.

**Recommendation: Option 3.** Skip DOM unit tests. The pure-math rungs (1-4) already cover the hard logic. DOM renderers stay thin bridges from layout data → elements. Playwright E2E in Step 10 catches actual visual regressions. This matches the established codebase pattern and avoids tests that give false confidence.

Shall I proceed with Step 5 (Browse Mode Rendering) under this approach — implementation without DOM unit tests, relying on Steps 1-4's math coverage and Step 10's E2E for validation?

Now Step 6 — Explore Mode Rendering. This is where a user clicks a file node and the view shifts to show that node's card with full symbol details, its direct neighbors at summary detail, and dependency connections piercing membrane boundaries.

**Fork-in-the-road: How does Explore Mode activation work in the Membrane Map?**

The execution plan describes 4 modes (Browse/Explore/Compare/Path), but there's a fundamental architectural question about how modes relate:

**In the existing codebase:**

- Circuit Board is ALWAYS browse-mode (progressive disclosure treemap) — clicking a file selects it, clicking a directory expands it. There's no "focal node" rendering within Circuit Board.
- Local Map is the focal/explore view — when you select a node, it renders that node's local neighborhood. It has exploration mode (single FROM) and path mode (FROM → TO).

**The Membrane Map unifies both.** So the question is:

1. **Mode transition trigger**: When the user clicks a file tile in browse mode, should it:
   - **(A)** Stay in browse mode with the tile highlighted, and the user must explicitly switch to "Explore" (via a button/hotkey)?
   - **(B)** Automatically transition to explore mode, expanding the relevant membranes to show the focal node at full detail with neighbors?
   - **(C)** Keep the spatial layout intact but overlay focal information (highlight the node, dim non-neighbors, show connection lines) — essentially browse mode + focal overlay?

2. **Spatial stability during mode transition**: If we go with B or C, the membrane layout should NOT change when entering explore mode — the nodes stay where they are, but their visual detail level changes. This is the key difference from Local Map (which does a completely different spatial layout). The Membrane Map's unique value is that you see the focal node IN CONTEXT of its spatial position within the codebase.

**Option C** is the most natural for a unified view — the treemap stays spatially stable, clicking a node adds a "focal spotlight" that modulates detail levels (from Step 3) and draws connections (Step 7), without rearranging everything. Double-clicking could open a dedicated expanded view if needed.

This is a genuine design fork. Option C would mean "Explore mode" isn't a separate renderer — it's the browse renderer with a focal node selected and detail levels applied. The rendering is the same DOM tree, just with different CSS classes (`.membrane-leaf--full`, `--summary`, `--badge`, `--hidden`) driven by `resolveDetailLevels()`.

Shall I proceed with **Option C** (browse + focal overlay), or do you prefer a distinct mode switch?

Made changes.

Or perhaps a single click expands the symbol pins so we can see them and the second click/tap causes the local map-like connectivity to 1-hop neighbors to be shown? Please propose a few more options for how this UX could be achieved (and consider the possibility; that, especially using things like the existing buttons on the Detail Panel, we could have more than one way to engage/disengage these distinct view modes)

GitHub Copilot:

Great question — this is exactly the kind of interaction design fork worth pausing on. Here are several options, each with different tradeoffs:

---

### Option A: Click → Select + Detail Panel, Double-click → Focal Overlay

- **Single click**: Selects the node (highlight border), opens Detail Panel sidebar. Treemap stays untouched.
- **Double-click**: Activates focal overlay — `resolveDetailLevels()` kicks in, dimming non-neighbors, symbol pins appear on the focal node, connection lines draw to 1-hop neighbors. Layout stays spatially stable.
- **Click elsewhere / Esc**: Deactivates focal overlay, back to browse.
- **Detail Panel "Focus" button**: Alternative path to activate focal overlay without double-click.

_Pro_: Clean separation — casual browsing never triggers heavy rendering. Matches the "click = select, double-click = act" convention.  
_Con_: Double-click is a discoverable problem — new users won't know to try it.

---

### Option B: Progressive Disclosure via Repeated Click

- **Click 1**: Selects node, opens Detail Panel, **and** expands the file tile in-place to show symbol pins (the tile grows vertically to reveal its exported symbols as small labeled rows).
- **Click 2** (within ~1.5s on same node): Draws 1-hop connections from those pins to neighbors. Dims distant nodes. Edge bundles on collapsed membranes become visible.
- **Click elsewhere**: Collapses pins, clears connections, back to browse.

_Pro_: Progressive — each click adds one layer of information. User learns by exploring.  
_Con_: The in-place tile expansion changes the layout slightly (tile grows), which wars with spatial stability. The time-window mechanic is invisible UX.

---

### Option C: Click → Select, Detail Panel Drives Mode

- **Single click**: Selects node + Detail Panel. No focal overlay. This is the ONLY click behavior on file tiles.
- **Detail Panel "Show Connections" button**: Activates focal overlay with connection lines to 1-hop neighbors. Pins appear on the focal node.
- **Detail Panel "Open in Local View" button** (existing): Still available as escape hatch to the full column-layout Local Map.
- **Keyboard shortcut** (e.g. `F` or `Enter` with node selected): Also activates focal overlay.

_Pro_: Click behavior is dead simple. Mode activation is explicit and discoverable (buttons have labels). Multiple entry points (button + keyboard).  
_Con_: Requires sidebar to be open. Two-click flow (click node, then click button) feels indirect when exploring rapidly.

---

### Option D: Click → Select + Pins, Detail Panel or Shortcut → Connections

A hybrid of B and C:

- **Single click**: Selects node, opens Detail Panel, **and** shows symbol pins inline on the tile (but no connection lines drawn yet). The tile doesn't resize — pins are just small dots along the left/right edges of the existing tile rect, appearing at the positions they'd connect from.
- **Detail Panel "Show Connections" button** or **keyboard `Enter`**: Draws connection lines from pins to neighbors. Dims distant nodes.
- **Click a different node**: That node becomes the new focal (pins + connections shift).
- **Click empty space / Esc**: Clears focal state.

_Pro_: Single click gives immediate spatial feedback ("this node has 4 outbound pins, 2 inbound") without the visual weight of full connection lines. The connection drawing is opt-in. Pins on edges don't disturb layout since they overlay the existing tile rect.  
_Con_: Pins without connections might confuse ("what do these dots mean?").

---

### Option E: Click → Focal Immediately (Local Map-like)

- **Single click on file tile**: Immediately activates focal overlay. The clicked node gets symbol pins, 1-hop neighbors get summary styling, connections draw, distant nodes dim. Detail Panel opens simultaneously.
- **Click a different file tile**: Transfers focal to the new node (spatially stable — just different pins/connections/dimming).
- **Click a membrane (directory) border or empty space**: Clears focal, back to browse.
- **Detail Panel buttons**: "Open in Local View" for full column layout, "Open in Circuit Board" preserved.

_Pro_: One click does everything. Fastest exploration flow. Matches the "the whole point of this view is to see connections in spatial context" philosophy.  
_Con_: No "light" selection state — every click triggers the full focal rendering, which may feel heavy if the user is just trying to browse.

---

### Option F: Context-Dependent (Collapsed vs. Expanded)

- **Click collapsed membrane tile**: Expands the membrane (current browse behavior, unchanged).
- **Click leaf file tile**: Selects + opens Detail Panel + shows inline pins (no connections yet).
- **Click a pin on the focal node**: Draws a single connection line from that specific pin to its target(s). Click another pin to add its connections too. This gives per-symbol exploration.
- **Detail Panel "Show All Connections"**: Draws all connections at once.
- **Esc / click empty space**: Clears.

_Pro_: Per-symbol exploration is uniquely powerful — "show me just what `handleClick` depends on" without the visual noise of all other connections. This is something neither Circuit Board nor Local Map does today.  
_Con_: Most complex interaction model. Pin click targets are small.

---

### My recommendation

**Option D** — it gives the best balance of:

- Immediate spatial feedback on click (pins appear, Detail Panel opens)
- Explicit opt-in for the heavier connection rendering (button or keyboard)
- Spatial stability throughout (pins overlay existing tile rects, no layout changes)
- Multiple entry points (Detail Panel button + keyboard shortcut)
- Clean deactivation (click elsewhere or Esc)

The per-symbol pin-click from Option F could layer on top later as a refinement.

Which of these resonates, or would you like to hybridize elements from multiple options?

GitHub Copilot:

This is a genuinely important insight. Let me restate it to make sure we're aligned, because it reframes the entire mode architecture.

---

### The Insight: Local Map = All Pins Active

The current Local Map renders a focal node with **every** public symbol visible, drawing connections from **all** of them to 1-hop neighbors in both directions. That's its fixed behavior — there's no way to say "show me just the connections from `handleClick`."

But if we model each symbol as independently **pinnable**, then:

| Pins Active         | What You See                                                                               |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **0 pins**          | Selected state — Detail Panel open, tile highlighted, no connections drawn                 |
| **1 pin**           | That symbol's connections only. Neighbors that don't interact with it are dimmed/hidden    |
| **N pins** (subset) | Union of those symbols' connections. A filtered view **that doesn't exist anywhere today** |
| **All pins**        | Every symbol's connections drawn → **this IS the existing Local Map**                      |

The Local Map isn't a mode. It's the logical endpoint of progressive pin activation. The Membrane Map can reach it organically through incremental clicks rather than via a mode switch.

### What This Means for Architecture

The original execution plan had 4 discrete modes (Browse / Explore / Compare / Path). This insight collapses the first three into a **continuous spectrum**:

```
Browse ──→ Selected ──→ Partial Pins ──→ All Pins (≡ Local Map)
  │            │              │                │
  │         Detail          Filtered        Full 1-hop
  │         Panel           connections     connectivity
  no focal   focal node     focal +         focal +
  node       highlighted    N connections   ALL connections
```

- **Browse**: No focal node. Pure treemap. Click directories to expand/collapse.
- **Selected**: Click a file tile. Detail Panel opens. Tile highlighted. Symbol list visible in tile (or Detail Panel). No connections drawn yet.
- **Partial Pins**: Click individual symbols in the list. Each click "pins" that symbol and draws its connections to neighbors. Neighbors that don't interact with any pinned symbol are dimmed.
- **All Pins**: Click "Select All" (or keyboard shortcut). Every symbol pinned. Full 1-hop connectivity drawn. This IS the Local Map — but rendered in spatial context rather than a column layout.

**Compare** also emerges naturally: if you pin symbols on node A, then Ctrl+click node B and pin symbols there, you see the union of both focal neighborhoods. No separate "compare mode" needed.

**Path** remains orthogonal — it's a pathfinding result (FROM → TO), not a pin state.

### Implementation Consequences

This means:

1. **No separate focal/explore renderer** (Step 6 as planned is absorbed into the browse renderer + pin state)
2. **The connection renderer** (Step 7) needs to operate per-symbol, not per-node — it draws connections for pinned symbols only
3. **The mode router** (Step 8) simplifies dramatically — there are only two states: browse (no focal) and focal (with pin set). Path mode is a third, triggered by pathfinding.
4. **The interaction layer** (Step 9) is where the pin toggle lives — click handlers on symbol rows

### Fork-in-the-Road Question

This is a significant enough redesign that the execution plan should be updated before proceeding. The key decision:

**Do we adopt the continuous pin-based model**, collapsing Steps 6/7/8 into a unified "focal overlay with per-symbol pins" implementation? Or do we keep discrete modes?

I strongly advocate for the continuous model — it's simpler (fewer renderers), more powerful (filtered views per-symbol), and more discoverable (each click teaches you something new). But it changes the remaining step structure enough to warrant your sign-off before I restructure the plan.

User: I'm inclined to agree but I want to ask one more thing: if we generalize this symbol pinning mechanism sufficiently, would it be possible to, through the activation of symbols, begin producing local map-like displays which are multi-focused (akin to the BFS search feature we currently have)? That is: If I click my way down to a directory with a few file nodes, click one single file node in there to expand its symbols, pin a single symbol to hide all non-connected nodes and show connected nodes from differing directories, then pin a related symbol in one of the hops away, should I then see the same phenomenon of nodes which _are_ connected to the secondary pinned symbol appearing as well, all while respecting the left (green pin) = inbound dependency and right (blue pin) = outbound dependency? This would likely mean that vertical rearrangement of active nodes would be necessary to accommodate the activation of additional nodes and symbols dynamically.

GitHub Copilot:

This is the right question to ask before committing to the architecture. The answer reveals a real tension.

### What Works: Multi-Focal Pinning in Spatial Context

If you pin symbol `A.handleClick` and it connects to `B.validate`, then you pin `B.validate` and it connects to `C.checkSchema` — **all three nodes are already positioned in the treemap**. The connections draw as lines piercing membranes. Nodes without any connection to any pinned symbol dim away. This works because you're adding more visibility, not rearranging space.

The green/blue (inbound/outbound) pin directionality works too — each symbol's pins mark which side connections enter/exit from, and the connection lines follow those anchors regardless of where the node sits spatially.

### What Breaks: Vertical Rearrangement

Here's where the treemap's spatial layout and the Local Map's topological layout are genuinely incompatible.

The treemap positions nodes by **filesystem weight and hierarchy** — squarify assigns rectangles based on file count and directory nesting. The Local Map positions nodes by **dependency topology** — columns represent dependency direction, vertical ordering within columns represents connection density.

If you start rearranging nodes vertically in the treemap to accommodate multi-hop chains, you've done one of two things:

1. **Moved nodes out of their membrane containers** — breaking the spatial hierarchy that IS the treemap's reason for existing
2. **Rearranged nodes within their membranes** — which might work for small membranes but creates chaos when hop N+1 is in a completely different corner of the codebase

The Local Map's column layout exists precisely because multi-hop exploration needs **topological arrangement**, not spatial arrangement. When you're tracing `A → B → C → D`, what matters is the chain order, not that A lives in shared and D lives in scripts.

### The Honest Design Answer

The pin model creates a **spectrum**, but that spectrum has a natural transition point:

```
Spatial (treemap)                          Topological (columns)
├── Browse (no pins)                       ├── Multi-hop chain
├── Selected (0 pins, Detail Panel)        ├── Path mode (FROM → TO)
├── Partial pins (1-N symbols, 1 node)     │
├── All pins (≡ 1-hop Local Map)           │
├── Multi-focal (pins on 2+ nodes)         │
│                                          │
└── Transition point ─────────────────────►┘
    "I'm now doing chain exploration"
```

**1-hop from any number of focal nodes** stays spatial. The treemap handles it because you're adding overlay information (connections, dimming) without disturbing positions.

**Multi-hop chains** need topological arrangement. Trying to keep them spatial would produce a worse version of the Local Map — connections zigzagging across the full treemap with nodes too far apart to see together.

### Proposed Resolution

The Membrane Map doesn't try to become the Local Map. Instead:

1. **Pins on a single focal node**: Stay in the treemap. Connections draw to wherever neighbors are. Dim non-connected nodes.
2. **Multi-focal (pins on multiple nodes at the same depth)**: Still treemap. Union of connections.
3. **Chain exploration trigger**: When you pin a symbol on a **neighbor** (a node you reached via connection, not via browsing), the view recognizes you're building a chain and offers a transition — either (a) opens the Local Map with that chain pre-populated, or (b) switches to an inset column layout within the membrane viewport showing just the chain's nodes in topological order.

Option (b) would be the "membrane-local-hybrid" — a small topological inset that appears when you start chaining, growing as you add hops, while the treemap background dims. This could be the Membrane Map's novel contribution: you see WHERE the chain lives (dimmed treemap background) AND the chain itself (topological inset). But that's a later refinement, not Commit 1.

### For Commit 1

I'd recommend implementing the pin model through **1-hop multi-focal** (stays spatial), with the Detail Panel's existing "Open in Local View" button as the escape hatch for multi-hop chain exploration. The chain-exploration-within-membrane refinement can come in a later commit once the basic pin model proves itself.

This keeps the scope tractable while preserving the full design vision as a documented next step. Shall I update the execution plan accordingly and proceed?

I think that for the vast majority of multi-hop cases, this will actually prove _doable_. I've now re-read the entire Day 79 conversation. You're right — I was too conservative about multi-hop feasibility in the membrane view. Let me correct that position and explain why.

## The "Common Membrane" Solves Multi-Hop

Looking back at my own Day 79 ASCII diagram of the BFS path `①check-symbols → ②symbolReferences → ③githubSlugger → ④githubSluggerRegex`:

```
┌─ ROOT ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                                 │
│  ┌─ scripts/slopcop/ ┐   ┌─ packages/shared/src/tooling/ ──┐  │
│  │                    │   │                                  │  │
│  │  ① check-symbols ──────→ ② symbolReferences              │  │
│  │     .ts  (FROM)    │   │       │                          │  │
│  │                    │   │       ↓                          │  │
│  └────────────────────┘   │    ③ githubSlugger               │  │
│                           │       │                          │  │
│                           │       ↓                          │  │
│                           │    ④ githubSluggerRegex (TO)     │  │
│                           │                                  │  │
│                           └──────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                                                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Three of four hops are **inside the same membrane** (`tooling/`). Only one hop crosses a boundary. That's not coincidence — it's how real codebases work. Functionally related files cluster organizationally. The membrane makes this immediately visible: hops ②→③→④ are trivial same-directory traversals; hop ①→② is the architecturally significant cross-package step. **The column layout treats every hop equally; the membrane shows you which hops matter.**

My concern was "the narrative flow is non-obvious" — but that's solved by:

- **Numbered hop badges** (①②③④) on each focal node
- **Animated path pulse** traveling along connections in sequence
- **Path breadcrumb bar** at the top as a linear narrative supplement

The membrane's advantage (structural distance as visual boundary-crossing) is something the column layout **can never provide**. And the column layout's advantage (linear narrative) is fully restored by the numbered badges + breadcrumb.

## When Multi-Hop Gets Hard (But Still Works)

Even the worst case — hops scattered across completely different top-level directories — is handled by the common membrane (LCA) concept from Day 79:

```
┌─ ROOT (LCA) ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                                            │
│  ┌─ packages/server/ ─┐  ┌─ packages/shared/ ─┐  ┌─ scripts/slopcop/ ─┐  │
│  │                     │  │                     │  │                     │  │
│  │  ① handler.ts ──────────→ ② types.ts ────────────→ ③ check.ts       │  │
│  │                     │  │                     │  │                     │  │
│  └─────────────────────┘  └─────────────────────┘  └─────────────────────┘  │
│                                                                            │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The LCA membrane expands to contain all path nodes. Every boundary crossing is visible. It's information-dense but **not paradoxical**. The numbered badges restore the linear narrative.

The long-chain edge case (8+ hops spanning many directories) degrades **identically** in both column and membrane layouts — too much visual noise either way. But at least the membrane keeps everything spatially contextualized.

## Revised Position on Q2

Old position (Day 80): "Path mode might use column layout or membrane spatial layout — decision deferred."

New position: **Path mode should use the membrane substrate.** The column layout's narrative advantage is fully recoverable via numbered badges + breadcrumb bar + animated pulse. The membrane's structural context is unrecoverable in the column layout. The continuous pin model means Path mode is just "the currently active path highlighted on the membrane" — not a separate rendering mode at all.

## Updated Interaction Model: Continuous Pin Spectrum

Synthesizing your Day 80 insight ("Local Map = All Pins Active") with the multi-hop reassessment:

```
Browse (0 pins)     →  Selected (detail panel)  →  Pinned symbols  →  Path highlighted
   │                         │                          │                    │
   ▼                         ▼                          ▼                    ▼
 All collapsed          File card selected        Symbol connections     BFS chain traced
 Aggregate tiles        Detail panel shown        drawn on membrane      with numbered hops
 No connections         No connections yet         Pin count = N          Path pulse animation
```

There are no discrete "modes." There's a **continuous state**:

- `pinnedSymbols: Set<{nodeId, symbolName}>` — every pinned symbol's connections are drawn
- `activePath?: PathResult` — if a path is traced, its hops get numbered badges and a pulse
- The rendering is always the same membrane treemap; what changes is which connections are visible and at what fidelity

"Local Map" emerges when all symbols on a focal node are pinned. "Compare" emerges when symbols on two nodes are pinned. "Path" emerges when a BFS result is active. No mode picker needed — the UI state flows continuously.

User: One other thing that remains to be figured out (based on what I can currently envision) is _when to activate the detail panel_. If we want symbol pinning to occur via tap/click and progressively reveal more of the software's internal connectivity, we stand to have some real annoyance with each subsequent click bringing up the detail panel for the node that is having a symbol pinned on at some arbitrary number of hops away.

Additionally, state preservation in the URL for sharing. I want to send a colleague a URL and have their screen show my version of this free-exploration local-map-like display, with a series of symbols pinned showing how various data flows through the application. We talked a little bit about the possibility of using `lz-string` if we wanted to JSON-ify and max out on what could be shared via query params. Worth thinking about and planning for in any case.

Finally, we need to think about what happens as a result of dependencies which ultimately whip back around to a node which already is rendered in the screen presently. The existing local map handles this by simply duplicating the nodes. But once Membrane Map is wrapping common nodes in single directories, we have some extreme tension when it comes to displaying dependencies from left-to-right. There will exist little cyclic loops through various files and symbols in certain situations.

All of that is to say: the idea of freely exploring via pinning has genuine visual layout problems that it will have to solve, and we should expect of ourselves an ASCII drawing showing how we might resolve such tensions.

GitHub Copilot:

Three genuine design tensions worth working through carefully. Let me take them one at a time with diagrams.

## 1. Detail Panel Activation: Hierarchical Click Targets

The solution is to separate **card selection** from **symbol pinning** at the click-target level:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  handler.ts                ← CARD BODY      │ ← Click here = select (detail panel updates)
│                                              │
│  ● processRequest         ← SYMBOL PIN      │ ← Click here = toggle pin (detail panel unchanged)
│  ● validateInput          ← SYMBOL PIN      │
│  ● formatResponse         ← SYMBOL PIN      │
│  ■ Internals                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The exploration flow:

```
Step 1: Click A's card body
  → A selected, detail panel = A, card expands to show symbol pins
  → No connections drawn yet

Step 2: Click A.login pin (●)
  → Pin toggled ON, A.login's connections appear as SVG paths
  → Detail panel stays on A  ← KEY: pin click ≠ card click

Step 3: Connection arrow reaches B (possibly inside collapsed membrane)
  → B's card shows a small "1 arriving" indicator

Step 4: Click B's card body
  → B selected, detail panel = B, B's card expands to show symbol pins
  → A.login connections still visible (pins persist)

Step 5: Click B.getUser pin (●)
  → Pin toggled ON, B.getUser connections also appear
  → Detail panel stays on B
```

**The detail panel follows the last-clicked CARD BODY.** Pin clicks never change the detail panel. This means you can pin 8 symbols across 4 files and the detail panel stays anchored to whichever card you last explicitly selected.

**Edge case — collapsed membranes:** When a pinned symbol's connection reaches a collapsed membrane, the membrane boundary shows a small arrival indicator (dot or count badge on the border). Clicking the membrane expands it (existing behavior), revealing the target card, which can then be selected and have its symbols pinned.

## 2. URL State: Hybrid Readable + lz-string

The state to serialize:

```typescript
interface ShareableMembraneState {
  exp: string[]; // expanded directory paths
  sel: string | null; // selected node (detail panel)
  pins: Array<{ n: string; s: string }>; // pinned {nodeId, symbolName}
  path?: { from: string; to: string }; // active BFS endpoints
  t: [number, number, number]; // transform [k, x, y]
}
```

**Hybrid strategy:**

For simple states (1-3 pins, few expanded dirs), use readable query params:

```
?exp=shared/src,scripts&sel=types.ts&pin=types.ts:IConfig,handler.ts:processRequest&t=0.8,120,50
```

When the serialized state exceeds ~1500 chars (reasonable URL safety margin), switch to lz-string:

```
?s=NoIgxgbiBcoA4FMBOBLALgSwHYHMIBsB6E...
```

The client deserializes by checking: does the URL have `?s=`? If so, decompress via `lzstring.decompressFromEncodedURIComponent()`. Otherwise, parse readable params.

`lz-string` is [6KB gzipped, zero dependencies, MIT licensed](https://github.com/pieroxy/lz-string). It's a good fit — already used in similar tools (Excalidraw, TypeScript Playground).

This should be a sub-item of Step 9 (controller integration), where URL state persistence is already planned.

## 3. Cycles and Already-Rendered Nodes: The Hard One

The Local Map handles `A→B→C→A` by **duplicating nodes**:

```
Local Map (current):
┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
│ Dependencies│  │   Center    │  │ Dependents  │
│             │  │             │  │             │
│  C.ts  ────────→  A.ts  ────────→  B.ts       │
│  B.ts  ────────→          ────────→  C.ts ←DUP│
│             │  │             │  │             │
└─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘
       ↑ C appears in both columns — duplicate
```

The Membrane Map **can't duplicate** because each file has exactly one spatial position. But it doesn't need to — the key insight is that **the membrane decouples spatial position from dependency direction.** Direction is communicated by **arrows on connections**, not by left-vs-right column placement.

### Case 1: Simple Cycle Within One Membrane

`A.login → B.getUser → C.validate → A.check` — all in `services/`:

```
┌─ services/ ─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                      │
│   ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐│
│   │ A.ts       │    │ B.ts       │    │ C.ts       ││
│   │ 📌login ━━━━━━▶ │ 📌getUser ━━━━▶ │ 📌validate ││
│   │   check ◀━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━ ││
│   │            │    │            │    │            ││
│   └────────────┘    └────────────┘    └────────────┘│
│                                                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The return connection from `C.validate → A.check` is just a line going rightward-from-C that curves back to A. Three pins, three connections, one visible loop. Not paradoxical — informative. The user sees immediately: "these three files form a cycle."

### Case 2: Cross-Membrane Cycle

`A (services/) → B (shared/) → C (shared/) → A`:

```
┌─ ROOT ──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                          │
│  ┌─ services/ ──────────┐   ┌─ shared/ ───────────────┐│
│  │                       │   │                          ││
│  │  ┌────────────┐      │   │  ┌────────────┐         ││
│  │  │ A.ts       │      │   │  │ B.ts       │         ││
│  │  │ 📌login ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━▶ │ 📌getUser   │         ││
│  │  │   check    │      │   │  │   │         │         ││
│  │  │    ▲       │      │   │  └───┼─────────┘         ││
│  │  │    │       │      │   │      │                    ││
│  │  └────┼───────┘      │   │  ┌───▼─────────┐         ││
│  │       │               │   │  │ C.ts        │         ││
│  │       │               │   │  │ 📌validate  │         ││
│  │       │               │   │  └───┼──────────┘        ││
│  └───────┼───────────────┘   └──────┼───────────────────┘│
│          │                          │                     │
│          └──────────────────────────┘                     │
│              C.validate → A.check                        │
│              (pierces shared/ out, services/ in)         │
│                                                          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The return arrow crosses two membrane boundaries. **The number of boundaries crossed IS the visual signal** — this cycle involves a cross-organizational dependency, which is architecturally notable.

### Case 3: The Diamond (Common Dependency, Not a Cycle)

`A → B, A → C, B → D, C → D` — D is a common dependency reached via two paths:

```
┌─ services/ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                              │
│  ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐                           │
│  │ A.ts       │    │ B.ts       │                           │
│  │ 📌parse ━━━━━━▶ │ 📌transform━━━━━━━┐                    │
│  │ 📌load  ━━━━━━┐ │            │      │                    │
│  └────────────┘  │ └────────────┘      │                    │
│                  │                      ▼                    │
│                  │ ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐         │
│                  │ │ C.ts       │    │ D.ts       │         │
│                  └▶│ 📌validate━━━━━▶│  serialize  │         │
│                    │            │    │            │         │
│                    └────────────┘    └────────────┘         │
│                                                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

D appears **once**. Two arrows arrive at D from different sources. No duplication needed — the spatial layout naturally shows the fan-in pattern. This is strictly **better** than the Local Map, which would show D in two separate places (once as B's dependent, once as C's dependent), obscuring the fact that it's the same file.

### Case 4: Bidirectional Dependency (A ↔ B)

`A.login → B.verify` AND `B.session → A.check`:

```
┌─ services/ ─────────────────────────────────┐
│                                              │
│   ┌────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐ │
│   │ auth.ts        │   │ session.ts       │ │
│   │ 📌login  ━━━━━━━━▶ │    verify        │ │
│   │    check ◀━━━━━━━━ │ 📌session        │ │
│   │                │   │                  │ │
│   └────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘ │
│                                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Two connections, opposite directions, visually clear. The Day 79 "French Corset" for same-file self-references works identically — `A.sym1 → A.sym2` is an arrow that leaves one pin and curves back to another pin on the same card.

### Case 5: The Actually Hard One — Dense Fan-Out Returning to the Focal Node

This is the stress test. You pin `A.init` and it connects outward to 6 files. You follow 3 of them (pinning symbols on B, C, D). Two of those connect BACK to A at different symbols:

```
┌─ app/ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                              │
│   ┌─────────────────┐                                       │
│   │ A.ts (FOCAL)    │                                       │
│   │ 📌init ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━┐                     │
│   │   configure ◀━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━┼━━━━━━━━━━━┐         │
│   │   shutdown ◀━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━┐     │           │         │
│   │   getState  ━━━▶ (to E,F)   │     │           │         │
│   └─────────────────┘           │     │           │         │
│                                  │     │           │         │
│   ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────┼─────┼──┐  ┌─────┼──────┐  │
│   │ B.ts         │  │ C.ts     │     │  │  │ D.ts│      │  │
│   │ 📌setup ━━━━━━━▶│ 📌run ━━━┘     │  │  │ 📌load     │  │
│   │              │  │ stop ━━━━━━━━━━┘  │  │  format━━━━┘  │
│   └──────────────┘  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────┘  │
│                                                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Here: `A.init → B, C, D`. `C.run → A.configure`. `C.stop → A.shutdown`. `D.format → A.configure`.

Three return arrows converge on A. The card A has multiple inbound connections arriving at different symbols. This is **dense but honest**. Each connection is a labeled arrow with a clear source and target pin. The viewer can trace: "C.run feeds back into A.configure, and D.format also feeds into A.configure — those two functions have shared upstream pressure."

**Would this benefit from edge bundling?** The two connections to `A.configure` (from C.run and D.format) could bundle at the pin level — a "2 inbound" badge on the `configure` pin, expandable to show the individual sources. This would be a pin-level analog of the membrane-level edge bundling from Step 4/8.

---

## Design Decisions Summary

| Concern                         | Proposed Resolution                                                                                                                                                                       |
| ------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Detail panel activation**     | Click on card body = select (detail panel updates). Click on symbol pin = toggle pin only (no detail panel change). Two distinct click targets, same DOM element.                         |
| **URL sharing**                 | Hybrid: readable query params for simple states, `lz-string` compression for complex (>1500 char) states. Detect via `?s=` prefix. Sub-item of Step 9.                                    |
| **Cycles / return connections** | No node duplication. Direction communicated by arrows, not position. Cycles are visible loops — a feature, not a bug. Fan-in to a single node shows clearly that multiple paths converge. |

### Fork-in-the-Road: Pin-Level Edge Bundling

Case 5 above reveals a question I don't want to silently resolve: **should connections bundle at the PIN level, not just at the membrane level?**

If `A.configure` has 6 inbound connections from 4 different files, we could either:

- **A**: Draw all 6 arrows individually (honest but potentially cluttered)
- **B**: Bundle at the pin: show "6 inbound" badge on the `configure` pin, hover to fan out individual connections
- **C**: Bundle by source membrane: connections from the same directory merge into one line until they reach A's pin

This interacts with the edge bundling already planned in Step 8. My instinct is **A for low-count (≤4), B for high-count (>4)**, with the threshold configurable. But I want your read before encoding that assumption.

(Not that we should add spatial dimensions per se, but I just want you to appreciate how much free exploration/expansion of pinned symbols can be a layout problem).

### 1. Detail panel activation — resolved

Card body click = select (detail panel). Symbol pin click = toggle pin only. Agreed.

### 2. URL state — always lz-string, comprehensive

You're right — hybrid is wrong. One serialization strategy, period. Always `lz-string.compressToEncodedURIComponent()` into a single `?s=` param. The `ExplorerShareableState` covers **everything**:

```ts
interface ExplorerShareableState {
  view: "circuit" | "local" | "force" | "sources" | "membrane";
  nodeId?: string;
  // Membrane state
  membrane?: {
    expanded: string[]; // expanded directory IDs
    pins: PinEntry[]; // symbol-level pins
    transform: { x: number; y: number; k: number };
    path?: { from: string; to: string };
  };
  // Force Graph state
  force?: {
    archetypes: Record<string, boolean>; // checkbox states
    showRelatedDocs: boolean;
  };
  // Local Map state
  local?: {
    pinnedSymbol?: { nodeId: string; symbol: string };
  };
  // Global (all views)
  filters: { showTests: boolean; showAssets: boolean; showRelatedDocs: boolean };
  tuning: PersistedTuning; // full tuning panel state
}
```

Backward compatibility: existing `?view=&node=` URLs get a one-time parse-and-redirect to `?s=`. This is a Step 9 (integration) work item.

### 3. Pin directionality and cycles — the real problem

You're right that I underappreciated this. After reading the full pin system, here's what I now see:

**The Local Map's spatial grammar:**

```
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │           file-a.ts             │
  │                                 │
  │  ●green  fn_alpha      ●blue   │
  │  ●green  fn_beta       ●blue   │
  │  ⬛      Internals             │
  │                                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────┘
     LEFT                    RIGHT
     inbound pins            outbound pins
     "I receive"             "I produce"
```

CSS confirms this: `.symbol-anchor.hub.inbound { left: -6px }`, `.symbol-anchor.hub.outbound { right: -6px }`. The grid is literally `16px | 1fr | 16px` — [in-dot][label][out-dot]. The `offsetToEdge` function in connections.ts encodes this algebraically: outbound = `centerX + distance`, inbound = `centerX - distance`.

**Why the column layout makes this invisible:**

```
  UPSTREAM (deps)        CENTER             DOWNSTREAM (users)
  ┌─────────┐           ┌─────────┐           ┌─────────┐
  │    ●out─┤───→───────┤─●in     │           │         │
  │         │           │    ●out─┤───→───────┤─●in     │
  └─────────┘           └─────────┘           └─────────┘

  All connections flow LEFT → RIGHT by construction.
  Outbound pins face RIGHT toward the next column.
  Inbound pins face LEFT toward the previous column.
  The spatial axis IS the dependency axis. Zero tension.
```

**Why the treemap breaks it — Case taxonomy:**

_Case 1: a→b, a LEFT of b (the happy case)_

```
  ┌─────────┐       ┌─────────┐
  │  a.ts   │       │  b.ts   │
  │    ●out─┤──→────┤─●in     │    ✓ pins face each other
  └─────────┘       └─────────┘
```

_Case 2: a→b, a RIGHT of b (outbound faces away from target)_

```
  ┌─────────┐       ┌─────────┐
  │  b.ts   │       │  a.ts   │
  │  ●in    │       │    ●out─┤──┐  a's outbound goes RIGHT
  └────┬────┘       └─────────┘  │  but b is to the LEFT
       │                         │
       └─────────────────────────┘  ← must wrap ALL the way around
```

The connection leaves a's RIGHT edge, goes further right, curves around, and arrives at b's LEFT edge from the left. This is the **inter-card French Corset**.

_Case 3: Cycle a→b AND b→a, a LEFT of b_

```
  ┌─────────┐       ┌─────────┐
  │  a.ts   │       │  b.ts   │
  │    ●out─┤──→────┤─●in     │    ✓ a→b fine
  │  ●in    │       │    ●out─┤──┐  b→a: b's outbound goes RIGHT
  └────┬────┘       └─────────┘  │  but a is LEFT
       │                         │
       └─────────────────────────┘  ← inter-card French Corset
```

ONE direction works, the reverse ALWAYS wraps. There's no arrangement where both work.

_Case 4: a→b, a BELOW b (vertical adjacency)_

```
  ┌─────────┐
  │  b.ts   │
  │  ●in    │     b is above a. a's outbound exits RIGHT.
  └────┬────┘     b's inbound is on LEFT.
       │          Connection must leave a's right, curve UP
  ┌────┴────┐     and around to b's left.
  │  a.ts   │
  │    ●out─┤──→ ... goes right, then where?
  └─────────┘
```

Vertical adjacency is even worse — neither L/R pin alignment helps because the spatial relationship is on the WRONG AXIS entirely.

**The fundamental impossibility:**

The Local Map's design language uses the X-axis for two things:

1. Pin placement (in=left, out=right)
2. Dependency direction (sources=left, targets=right)

These are coupled in the column layout. In the treemap, position is determined by HIERARCHICAL CONTAINMENT (squarify), which is orthogonal to dependency direction. The X-axis is consumed by the treemap. There is no remaining spatial axis for dependency direction.

You said "additional spatial dimensions would plausibly be required." I agree. Here are the options I see, each making a different trade:

---

**Option A: Direction lives on the LINE, not the pin**

Abandon the left-green/right-blue positional grammar entirely for the membrane context. Each symbol has a single neutral pin (or a pair that's aesthetically balanced but functionally equivalent). Direction is encoded in the connection itself:

```
  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
  │  a.ts    │       │  b.ts    │
  │  ○ fn_x ○│       │○ fn_y ○  │
  └──────────┘       └──────────┘
        ●═══════▶═══════●           a→b: arrowhead + gradient
        ●◀══════════════●           b→a: arrowhead + gradient

  Pins are on whichever edge FACES the partner.
  ▶ arrowhead + green→blue gradient encodes direction.
  Animated pulse optional.
```

Connection routing picks the shortest path — pin appears on whatever card edge faces the partner. No wrapping needed.

**Pro:** Zero routing paradoxes. All connections are short, direct, and unambiguous.  
**Con:** Discards the "glance at the pin side, know the direction" grammar. The membrane's visual language would differ from the Local Map's. Users moving between views lose muscle memory.

---

**Option B: Preserve L/R grammar, treat routing complexity as signal**

Keep green-left, blue-right. Accept that connections crossing the "wrong way" require dramatic curves. These dramatic curves ARE the information — they show "this dependency fights your organizational structure."

```
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/services/                                     │
  │  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │  a.ts    │       │  b.ts    │                 │
  │  │  ●  f  ●─┤─→─────┤─●  g  ● │   a→b ✓        │
  │  │  ●  h    │       │     i ●─┤─┐              │
  │  └────┬─────┘       └─────────┘ │               │
  │       │                         │  b→a wraps:   │
  │       └─────────────────────────┘  THE CORSET   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  When you see a giant curve, you KNOW the dependency
  direction fights the directory structure. That's signal.
```

**Pro:** Preserves the Local Map's design language perfectly. Routing complexity = coupling smell.  
**Con:** Visual clutter for cyclic-heavy codebases. Curves may cross many other cards, reducing readability. Needs sophisticated curve routing to avoid card intersections.

---

**Option C: Four-edge port routing (circuit board style)**

Each card has port slots on all 4 edges. Inbound ports are always green, outbound always blue, but they can appear on ANY edge. The routing algorithm assigns ports to minimize path length while maintaining color encoding.

```
  ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │           ●blue          │  ← top outbound port
  │  ┌──────────────┐       │
  │  │    a.ts      │       │
  ●──┤●green  ●blue├──●    │  ← L/R ports still available
  │  │              │       │
  │  └──────────────┘       │
  │           ●green         │  ← bottom inbound port
  └──────────────────────────┘

  Port assignment is per-connection:
  a→b where b is BELOW: use a's BOTTOM ●blue port
  c→a where c is ABOVE: use a's TOP ●green port
```

**Pro:** Short paths in any spatial arrangement. Preserves green=in/blue=out color grammar.  
**Con:** Symbol-level granularity only fits on L/R edges (top/bottom can only host node-level hubs). The familiar grid layout (`[●in][label][●out]`) only works horizontally. This is fundamentally the "each card is a chip with pins on all sides" metaphor — which, given our circuit board heritage, might actually be apt.

---

**Option D: Detail-level-aware hybrid**

The design changes based on detail level:

- **Full detail (focal card):** L/R pins exactly like the Local Map. The card IS a Local Map card.
- **Summary/Badge (neighbor tiles):** No symbol-level pins. Connections terminate at tile edges. The TILE's connection point is placed on whichever edge faces the focal card.

This means the routing paradox only manifests between two FULL-detail cards (multi-focal/path mode), not between a focal card and its neighbors.

```
  Single-focal scenario:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/                                              │
  │  ┌────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌────┐           │
  │  │dep1├──┤●in focal.ts ●out├──┤dep2│           │
  │  └────┘  │  fn_a        ●──┤  └────┘           │
  │          │  fn_b            │     ↑              │
  │          └──────────────────┘  tile can receive  │
  │                                on any edge       │
  │           ┌────┐                                  │
  │           │dep3│  ← tile below can receive on top │
  │           └────┘                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Focal card has L/R grammar. Tiles have no fixed pin side.
  Routing is always focal-edge → nearest tile-edge.
  The paradox only re-emerges with MULTIPLE focal cards.
```

**Pro:** Preserves L/R grammar where it matters most (the card you're examining). Routing is trivial for single-focal. Matches the progressive disclosure philosophy.  
**Con:** Multi-focal and path mode re-encounter the full problem. Two different visual grammars (L/R for focal, any-edge for tiles) might confuse.

---

**My read on the trade-offs:**

The fundamental dimension deficit is real and unfixable — you can't encode both hierarchy (treemap) and dependency direction (L/R) on two axes when they're independent dimensions. Every option sacrifices something.

I lean toward **Option D** as the default with **Option A** as the fallback for multi-focal/path mode — but I'm not confident. This is Q7. Your call.

User: Believe it or not, Option B has my attention the very most. I am most curious how we can mitigatge the visual difficulties with Option B, because I think an inter-card corset is the most visually understandable and truthful of what we've seen in the options presented. You correctly indicate the need for sophisticated curve routing to avoid card intersections (asterisk: is there some way that the concept of "wired around the back" could be leveraged to make it less visually cluttered while still readable as things which are tied together in a particular order?)

GitHub Copilot:

Option B with the "wired around the back" generalization is a genuinely beautiful idea. Let me develop it.

## The Two-Layer PCB Model

The self-loop French Corset already establishes a visual precedent: "this connection goes _around the back of the card_." The user can read it as a loop without it dominating the card face. What if we generalize this to ALL backward-facing connections?

**The principle:** Every connection is classified as either a **front trace** or a **back trace** based on a single test:

> Does the outbound pin naturally face the target's inbound pin?

```
  FRONT TRACE (outbound faces target):
  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
  │  a.ts    │       │  b.ts    │
  │     out●─┤══▶════┤─●in     │     Bézier on top, full opacity
  └──────────┘       └──────────┘     a's RIGHT faces b's LEFT ✓


  BACK TRACE (outbound faces AWAY from target):
  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐
  │  b.ts    │       │  a.ts    │
  │  ●in     │       │     out●─┤     a's RIGHT faces away from b's LEFT
  └──────────┘       └──────────┘     This becomes a back trace
```

The classification is trivially computable: if `outboundPin.x < inboundPin.x`, front trace. Otherwise, back trace.

## What Back Traces Look Like

Three visual differentiators, rendering **beneath** the card layer (lower z-index):

```
  FRONT TRACES                    BACK TRACES
  ─────────────                   ─────────────
  Full opacity (0.5-0.7)          Low opacity (0.12-0.18)
  Solid stroke                    Dashed stroke  ╌╌╌╌
  On top of cards                 Behind cards (z-index)
  Blue→Green gradient             Same gradient, just faint
  Standard Bézier                 Membrane-gutter routed
```

The back trace is _there_ — it's not hidden — but it's visually recessive, like the back-copper layer of a PCB viewed from the front. You can see it in the gaps between cards and at the edges of membranes.

## Via Markers

Each back trace has a **via** — a small distinctive marker at both endpoints where the trace "dives through the board." These are rendered ON TOP of cards (same z-index as pins) so they're always visible:

```
  ┌──────────────┐          ┌──────────────┐
  │  a.ts        │          │  b.ts        │
  │  ●in    out●─┤──◉      │      ◉──┤─●in│
  └──────────────┘  │       └──────────┤───┘
                    │                  │
                    ╰╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╯  ← back trace (low opacity, dashed,
                                            behind the cards)
  ◉ = via marker
  ● = normal pin
```

The via (◉) says: "a connection exists here, but it's routed on the back of the board." Visually it could be a ringed circle (like a PCB pad), subtly distinct from the solid-dot pin.

## Membrane-Gutter Routing

Back traces don't cut straight across — they route along the **gutters** formed by membrane borders, exactly like PCB traces follow channels between components:

```
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/services/                                    │
  │  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐   │
  │  │  auth.ts  │  │  api.ts   │  │  db.ts    │   │
  │  │ ●in  out●◉│  │ ●in  out●─┤═══┤─●in  out●│   │
  │  └─────┬─────┘  └───────────┘  └──────◉────┘   │
  │        │                              │         │
  │        ◉╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╯         │
  │     ↑ back trace routes along the bottom        │
  │       gutter of the membrane                    │
  │  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐                   │
  │  │  cache.ts │  │  log.ts   │                   │
  │  │ ●in  out●│  │ ●in  out●│                   │
  │  └───────────┘  └───────────┘                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  db.ts → auth.ts:  db's outbound faces RIGHT but auth is LEFT
  Front trace: impossible without crossing api.ts
  Back trace: routes along the bottom gutter, behind the cards
  Vias at both endpoints are visible on top
```

The gutters are naturally available — the squarify layout leaves gaps between cells (padding/margin), and membrane borders create clear channels. The back trace hugs these channels, never crossing card faces.

## Hover Promotion

Back traces are recessive by default. On interaction, they **promote** to full visibility:

```
  DEFAULT STATE:                      HOVER ON auth.ts (or its via):
  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐       ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐
  │  auth.ts  │  │  db.ts    │       │  auth.ts  │  │  db.ts    │
  │ ●in  out●◉│  │      ◉out●│       │ ●in  out●◉│  │      ◉out●│
  └─────┬─────┘  └──────┤────┘       └═════╤═════┘  └══════╤════┘
        │               │                  │               │
        ◉╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╯                  ◉═══════════════╯
        ↑ barely visible              ↑ promoted: full opacity, solid,
                                        animated pulse, on top of cards
```

This is the same hover-highlight pattern the Local Map already uses for symbol connections. The only difference: back traces start at near-invisible instead of normal opacity.

## The Full Cycle Case

The cycle a→b AND b→a now has a clear, honest visual representation:

```
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/                                          │
  │  ┌───────────┐       ┌───────────┐           │
  │  │  a.ts     │       │  b.ts     │           │
  │  │ ●in  out●═╪═══▶═══╪═●in  out●◉│           │
  │  │◉          │       │           │           │
  │  └──┬────────┘       └───────┬───┘           │
  │     │                        │               │
  │     ◉╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╯               │
  │                                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  a→b: FRONT trace (out● faces right toward ●in) — bold, on top
  b→a: BACK trace (out● faces right but a is left) — dashed, behind

  The asymmetry IS the information:
  a→b is "natural" (front). b→a is "fighting the layout" (back).
```

Both connections are present, readable, and distinguishable. The front/back asymmetry tells you something real: one direction aligns with the directory structure, the other doesn't.

## Vertical Adjacency

The case where a is below b — back traces handle this identically:

```
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/                          │
  │  ┌───────────┐               │
  │  │  b.ts     │               │
  │  │◉●in  out●│               │
  │  └─┤─────────┘               │
  │    │                          │
  │  ┌─┤─────────┐               │
  │  │ │  a.ts   │               │
  │  │ ╰◉   out●◉│               │
  │  │  ●in      │               │
  │  └───────────┤               │
  │              ╰╌╌╌ back trace  │
  │                 routes along  │
  │                 right gutter, │
  │                 up to b's via │
  └──────────────────────────────┘

  a→b: a's outbound faces RIGHT, b is ABOVE.
  Neither a front nor a trivially-backward case.
  Classification: outbound.x > inbound.x? If so, back trace.
  If not (b's inbound is further right), front trace with a vertical Bézier.
```

Actually, the classification needs one refinement for vertical cases. Let me sharpen it:

**Front trace if** the connection can be drawn as a clean Bézier without crossing the source card's body. Practically: `outboundPin.x < inboundPin.x` (the connection goes "rightward").

**Back trace if** `outboundPin.x >= inboundPin.x` (the connection would need to wrap around). For pure vertical cases, a vertical Bézier that goes outbound→right→curves up/down→inbound→left is still a front trace — it just has more curvature.

## What This Means for Implementation

The rendering engine needs:

1. **Classification function**: `isFrontTrace(outboundAnchor, inboundAnchor): boolean` — trivial.
2. **Front trace renderer**: Essentially what we'd build anyway — standard Bézier curves. Identical to the Local Map's connection renderer, adapted for absolute positions.
3. **Back trace renderer**: Membrane-gutter pathfinding. Given two anchor positions and the membrane hierarchy, find a path along membrane borders. Output: a polyline or spline. This is the new work.
4. **Via markers**: Small DOM elements or SVG circles at trace endpoints. Rendered in the pin z-layer.
5. **Two SVG layers**: `svg.front-traces` (z-index above cards) and `svg.back-traces` (z-index below cards). Hover promotion moves a path from back to front temporarily.

The gutter routing (item 3) is the most technically interesting piece. One approach: treat membrane borders as a rectilinear graph and find shortest paths along edges. Since we already have the full `MembraneLayout` tree with every rect, building this routing graph is straightforward.

You're making three precise corrections and I want to address each clearly.

### 1. Via placement — anchored to the actual pins

You're right. My diagrams were arbitrary. The via belongs _at the pin itself_ — it's a visual modifier on the existing green/blue dot, not a freestanding marker on a card edge. The outbound stub goes rightward from the blue pin. The inbound stub goes leftward from the green pin. The L/R visual language is perfectly preserved.

### 2. Dimming semantic collision

There are now potentially three distinct visual states:

- **Active**: this connection is relevant to what you're examining
- **Dimmed**: this connection exists but isn't relevant to the currently hovered/pinned symbol
- **Back trace**: this connection exists but routes "the wrong way"

Using dimming for BOTH "irrelevant" and "back trace" conflates two orthogonal meanings. That's a real problem.

### 3. Hidden-behind vs. dimmed — let me draw both

**Approach X: Nub Stubs (fully hidden path)**

The back-connection routing is completely invisible. You only see short stubs at each pin — the outbound pin has a small rightward curve vanishing "behind" the card, the inbound pin has a small leftward curve emerging "from behind." The path between them doesn't exist visually. It's behind everything — behind cards, behind membranes, behind the universe.

```
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/services/                                     │
  │                                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐   │
  │  │  auth.ts          │   │  db.ts            │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ●  validate  ●═══╪═══╪═●  query    ●    │   │  ← a→b front trace
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │     (normal Bézier)
  │  │  ╶●  connect     ●│   │●  getPool   ●╶   │   │
  │  │   ↑              │   │              ↑   │   │
  │  │  stub             │   │            stub  │   │
  │  │  "arrives          │   │  "departs         │   │
  │  │   from behind"     │   │   into behind"    │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ⬛  Internals    │   │  ⬛  Internals    │   │
  │  └───────────────────┘   └───────────────────┘   │
  │                                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  b→a (db.getPool → auth.connect): BACK CONNECTION
  - db's outbound ●╶  tiny rightward stub, vanishes immediately
  - auth's inbound ╶●  tiny leftward stub, appears from nothing
  - NO visible line between them. Zero visual clutter.
  - Connection is entirely implied by the paired stubs.
```

The stubs are literally the French Corset trick applied per-pin: a tiny SVG nub path that curves outward from the pin and fades/clips immediately. On the outbound side it goes rightward (respecting "I produce"). On the inbound side it goes leftward (respecting "I consume").

**How do you know which stubs pair together?** Hover on either stub reveals the full connection as a temporarily promoted trace:

```
  HOVER on db.ts "getPool" outbound stub:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/services/                                     │
  │                                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐   │
  │  │  auth.ts          │   │  db.ts            │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ●  validate  ●═══╪═══╪═●  query    ●    │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ◀╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌  │   │
  │  │  ╶●  connect     ●│   │●  getPool   ●╶   │   │
  │  │                   │   │           [HOVER] │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ⬛  Internals    │   │  ⬛  Internals    │   │
  │  └───────────────────┘   └───────────────────┘   │
  │                                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Promoted trace appears temporarily — faint dashed line
  showing the full back-connection path. Disappears on
  mouse-out.
```

When PINNED (not just hovered), the promoted trace stays visible until unpinned.

**Approach Y: Always-visible dimmed trace**

The full path is always rendered but at very low opacity, behind cards:

```
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ src/services/                                     │
  │                                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐   │
  │  │  auth.ts          │   │  db.ts            │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ●  validate  ●═══╪═══╪═●  query    ●    │   │
  │  │                   │   │                   │   │
  │  │  ╶●  connect     ●│ ╌╌╌╌●  getPool   ●╶  │   │
  │  │   ╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌  ╌  │              ╌  │   │
  │  │                  ╌│   │              ╌  │   │
  │  │  ⬛  Internals   ╌│   │  ⬛  Internals╌ │   │
  │  └──────────────────╌┘   └───────────────╌──┘   │
  │                      ╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌       │
  │        (back trace visible at ~15% opacity,       │
  │         routed along membrane gutter)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The trace is there, always, faintly. On hover it promotes to full visibility.

---

### My assessment

**Approach X wins.** Here's why:

1. **No dimming semantic collision.** Dimming keeps its ONE meaning: "irrelevant to current focus." Back connections aren't dimmed — they're _hidden by construction_. Two completely different visual mechanisms for two completely different concepts.

2. **Zero clutter by default.** A codebase with 50 cycles doesn't show 50 faint lines worming through the gutters at all times. It shows 50 pairs of subtle stubs — which are small enough to scan quickly.

3. **The stubs respect L/R perfectly.** Outbound stub goes right. Inbound stub goes left. The pin's directional grammar is not just preserved — it's _reinforced_ by the stub direction.

4. **Implementation is simpler.** No gutter routing algorithm needed for the default state. The stubs are tiny SVG nubs drawn locally at each pin — same technique as the French Corset. The promoted trace (shown on hover/pin) CAN use gutter routing, but it only needs to render for one connection at a time, which is far cheaper.

5. **The hover promotion is the dim-irrelevant system's natural complement.** Dim-irrelevant says "fade what's not about THIS symbol." Hover-promote says "reveal what's connected to THIS stub." They're different interactions on different targets with different visual outcomes. No collision.

**The one design question remaining:** How do stubs _visually signal_ that they're stubs (i.e., "there's something connected here that goes behind") versus normal pins that simply have no connections? Options:

```
  Normal unconnected pin:      ●         plain dot, no stub
  Front-connected pin:         ●════     dot with visible Bézier
  Back-connected pin (stub):   ●╶        dot with tiny departing curve
                            or ●◗        dot with a small directional wedge
                            or ●→        dot with a micro-arrowhead
                            or ●⊃        dot with a half-ring "portal" mark
```

I lean toward the tiny departing curve (the `●╶` in my diagrams) — it's the French Corset visual language in miniature, literally a 6-8px SVG path that curves away from the pin and fades out. Visually distinct from "no connection" (bare pin) and from "front connection" (full Bézier), without introducing any new iconography.

Do you want to go with Approach X + stub curves, or do you want to see any other variants explored before we lock this into Q7?

(which I think will actually be extremely difficult to do correctly and could reasonably be deferred until a future commit, not unlike the Namespace mode future commit). GitHub Copilot:

This is a clarifying insight that simplifies the architecture. The pathfinder is not a rendering mode — it's a **pin population strategy**. BFS produces an ordered set of `(nodeId, symbol)` pairs; those get injected into the pin set with hop-index metadata; the standard multi-pin renderer draws them. The numbered hop badges and breadcrumb bar are _annotations on the pin set_, not a separate rendering system.

This means Step 7 shrinks considerably. There's no "path highlighting system" — there's:

1. A BFS function (which already exists in the CLI as `inspect --from --to`)
2. A way to inject its results into the pin set with ordering metadata
3. Hop badges + breadcrumb bar as optional decorations on ordered pins

The omnisearch-to-symbol feature ("search for `buildStaticExplorer`, jump to its card, auto-pin that symbol") is the natural complement — another pin population strategy, this time user-driven rather than algorithm-driven.

And your point about CLI parity is well-taken: `inspect --from A --to B` and the Explorer pathfinder should produce equivalent results, just rendered differently. The pathfinder UI stays, but if it ever becomes an implementation obstacle, we know the pin model handles the same results regardless of how they were produced.

Here's my audit of both test suites:

### pin-state.test.ts (34 tests)

**Near-tautological (but acceptable as spectrum documentation):**

- "0 pins = Browse state" — re-asserts `EMPTY_PIN_SET.entries.length === 0` already proven by the constant definition and the first test. Same for `getPinnedNodeIds` returning size 0.
- "1 pin = Partial Pins state" — re-asserts that `addPin` produces 1 entry, already covered in "addPin adds a new entry".

These are borderline — they function as living documentation of the continuous spectrum concept. I'd keep them but they're not catching real bugs.

**Missing edge cases I'd add:**

1. **Links with no `sourceSymbol`/`targetSymbol`** — The implementation uses `if (link.sourceSymbol && sourceSymbols.has(...))`, so links without symbols should only be visible to wildcard pins. This is implicitly covered but not explicitly tested as a negative case.
2. **Duplicate link visibility** — verify a link matching BOTH source and target pins appears exactly once (not duplicated).

### routing.test.ts (18 tests)

**No tautological tests** — all assertions test meaningful computed values against nontrivial expectations. The `classifyTrace` suite's pin-radius edge case tests are particularly strong.

**Missing edge cases I'd add:**

1. **Degenerate geometry: same-point anchors** — Both pins at identical coordinates with radius 0. Could produce NaN/empty path in Bézier computation.
2. **Negative coordinates** — Pan/zoom transforms can produce negative viewport-relative coords. Should still route correctly.
3. **Extreme vertical separation** — Front trace with large Y delta to verify path length grows appropriately (not stuck at horizontal distance).
